Future global stability of Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria and vacuum for the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetimes Thanks: ‡ Partially supported by the NSF grant DMS-2408264 of the USA Thanks: † Partially supported through Royal Society Tata University Research Fellowship URF\R1\191409.
Robert M. Strain ‡, Martin Taylor †, Renato Velozo Ruiz †

Abstract
. In this work we study the general relativistic massless Boltzmann equation on Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker spacetimes with spatial topology 𝕋 3 \mathbb{T}^{3} in the linear and decelerated expanding regimes, where the scale factor is t 𝔮 t^{\mathfrak{q}} with 𝔮 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \mathfrak{q}\in[0,1] . The massless Boltzmann equation on these backgrounds admits non-stationary Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria of the form exp ⁡ ( − | t 2 ​ 𝔮 ​ p | ) \exp(-|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|) . For 0 ≤ 𝔮 ≤ 1 0\leq\mathfrak{q}\leq 1 , we prove future global-in-time existence and uniqueness of small perturbations of these equilibria in the case of hard ball interaction without symmetry assumptions. For 0 ≤ 𝔮 < 1 / 3 0\leq\mathfrak{q}<1/3 , we prove that the perturbation — measured in a suitable L p 2 L^{2}_{p} based energy norm — decays at the superpolynomial time-decay rate of t − 3 ​ 𝔮 ​ exp ⁡ ( − t 1 − 3 ​ 𝔮 ) t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}\exp(-t^{1-3\mathfrak{q}}) , whereas for 1 / 3 < 𝔮 ≤ 1 1/3<\mathfrak{q}\leq 1 we obtain the polynomial time-decay rate of t − 3 ​ 𝔮 t^{-3\mathfrak{q}} . In the borderline case 𝔮 = 1 / 3 \mathfrak{q}=1/3 , we show the time-decay of t − 3 ​ 𝔮 − c t^{-3\mathfrak{q}-c} with a uniform constant c > 0 c>0 . Finally, for 1 3 < 𝔮 ≤ 1 \frac{1}{3}<\mathfrak{q}\leq 1 , we prove future global-in-time existence and uniqueness of small perturbations of the vacuum solution on 𝕋 3 \mathbb{T}^{3} .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q20, 76P05, 35Q75, 82C40, 35B65, 83F05.

1.
Introduction

In this article,
we will study the long time dynamics of the general relativistic massless Boltzmann equation on homogeneous and isotropic cosmological models of the universe described by the Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) spacetimes

\mathcal{M}=I\times\Sigma,\qquad g=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)g_{\Sigma},

where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an open interval,
(\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})
is a constant curvature manifold, and
a\colon I\to(0,+\infty)
is called the scale factor. This article studies the future global non-linear stability of Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria on decelerated and linearly expanding FLRW spacetimes in which the constant curvature manifold is the 3-dimensional flat torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first future global-in-time stability result for the Boltzmann equation in the presence of spatial dependence and a non-trivial gravitational field.

1.1.
The Boltzmann equation in general relativity

Consider a
4
-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
, and let
\mathcal{P}\subset T\mathcal{M}
be the
mass-shell
of spacetime defined by

\mathcal{P}:=\big\{(x,p)\in T\mathcal{M}:g_{x}(p,p)=-m^{2}c^{2}\,\,\,\text{with $p$ future-directed}\big\},

where
c>0
is the speed of light, and
m\geq 0
is a fixed parameter describing the mass of the particles under consideration. The general relativistic Boltzmann equation on
(\mathcal{M},g)
concerns functions
F\colon\mathcal{P}\to[0,\infty)
.

Consider a local coordinate system
(t=x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
on
\mathcal{M}
, and let
(x^{\mu},p^{i})
be the corresponding conjugate coordinate system on
\mathcal{P}\subset T\mathcal{M}
, so that
(x^{\mu},p^{i})
corresponds to the point
p^{\mu}\partial_{x^{\mu}}|_{x}\in\mathcal{P}\subset T\mathcal{M}
, where
p^{0}
is defined in terms of
(x^{\mu},p^{i})
by the
mass shell relation

(1.1)

g_{\mu\nu}p^{\mu}p^{\nu}=-m^{2}c^{2}.

We use here the Einstein summation convention over repeated indices and adopt the convention that Greek indices range over 0, 1, 2, 3, and Latin indices range over 1, 2, 3. Spacetime points are typically denoted
(t,x)\in\mathcal{M}
, though in some expressions, which will be clear from the context,
x\in\mathcal{M}
is used for brevity. By a slight abuse, we will use the notation
p
both for points in
\mathcal{P}_{x}
, for fixed
x\in\mathcal{M}
, and for
p=(p^{1},p^{2},p^{3})
to denote elements of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Note that the latter parameterizes the former, with
p^{0}>0
defined by (
1.1
). We will also write
p^{\mu}
to denote the
\mu
component of
(p^{0},p^{1},p^{2},p^{3})
.

The Boltzmann equation on
(\mathcal{M},g)
then takes the form

(1.2)

p^{0}\partial_{t}F+p^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}F-p^{\mu}p^{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}F=C(F,F),

where

\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha}=\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\mu\beta}-\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}\big),

denote the Christoffel symbols of the metric
g
.

The collision operator
C(F,F)
in (
1.2
) is defined by

C(F,G)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}(q)\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}(q^{\prime})\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}(p^{\prime})W\big(F(q^{\prime})G(p^{\prime})-F(q)G(p)\big),

in terms of the volume form
d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}
in the fibers of the mass-shell
\mathcal{P}_{x}
given by

d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}(p)=\frac{\sqrt{-\mathrm{det}\,g}}{-p_{0}}dp^{1}dp^{2}dp^{3}.

We recall that indices are raised and lowered with respect to the metric
g
. Moreover, the transition rate
W=W(p,q|p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is defined by

(1.3)

W(p,q|p^{\prime},q^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}s\sigma(\varrho,\theta)\delta^{(4)}(p^{\mu}+q^{\mu}-p^{\prime\mu}-q^{\prime\mu})(-\mathrm{det}\,g)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

where
\sigma(\varrho,\theta)
is a scattering kernel measuring the interactions between particles. The conservation of energy and momentum due to the elastic collisions among particles is expressed by the fact that
W
is supported on
p
,
q
,
p^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}
, satisfying

(1.4)

p^{\mu}+q^{\mu}={p}^{\prime\mu}+{q}^{\prime\mu},

where the notation
p^{\mu}
,
q^{\mu}
, and
p^{\prime\mu}
,
q^{\prime\mu}
, indicates that these are pre and post-collisional momenta, respectively. Here the
relative momentum function

\varrho\,\colon\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{P}_{x}\to[0,+\infty)
is defined by

\displaystyle\varrho(p,q)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\sqrt{(p^{\mu}-q^{\mu})(p_{\mu}-q_{\mu})}=\sqrt{-2(p^{\kappa}q_{\kappa}+m^{2}c^{2})},

where
\varrho(p,q)
is well-defined since
p-q
is either a null or a spacelike vector. Define also the function
s\,\colon\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{P}_{x}\to[0,+\infty)
by

\displaystyle s(p,q)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}-(p^{\mu}+q^{\mu})(p_{\mu}+q_{\mu})=2\left(-p^{\kappa}q_{\kappa}+m^{2}c^{2}\right),

where
s\geq 0
since
p+q
is either a null or a timelike vector. Note that
s=\varrho^{2}+4m^{2}c^{2}.
We observe that
\varrho
and
s
are
collision invariants
in the sense that
\varrho(p,q)=\varrho(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
for all
p
,
q
,
p^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}
satisfying (
1.4
), and similarly for
s
. We define also the
scattering angle function

\theta\,\colon\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{P}_{x}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\cos(\theta(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}))\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}(p^{\mu}-q^{\mu})({p}_{\mu}^{\prime}-{q}_{\mu}^{\prime})/\varrho^{2}.

By the conservation of energy and momentum (
1.4
), it can be shown
that
\theta
and
\cos\theta
are well-defined.
We now remark that the transition rate (
1.3
) is
coordinate invariant
, and so, also is the collision operator.

1.2.
The Einstein–Boltzmann system and Maxwell–Jüttner FLRW

The Boltzmann equation in general relativity (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) is a fundamental kinetic model for the study of gravitational collisional systems. In the theory of general relativity, these systems are modelled by the
Einstein–Boltzmann system
, which consists of equations (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) coupled to the Einstein equations

(1.5)

Ric(g)_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}R(g)g_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}

where the energy-momentum tensor
T_{\mu\nu}
for the Boltzmann equation takes the form

T_{\mu\nu}[F]=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}F(t,x,p)p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\frac{\sqrt{-\mathrm{det}\,g}}{-p_{0}}dp^{1}dp^{2}dp^{3}.

The Einstein–Boltzmann system in the case of massless particles admits an explicit FLRW solution with spatial topology
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the scale factor
a(t)=(2t)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and with
F(t,x,p)=\exp(-|2tp|)
being a Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium. More generally, for any
\mathfrak{q}\geq\frac{1}{2}
, the
Einstein–massless Boltzmann–scalar field
system admits an FLRW solution with scale factor
a(t)\sim t^{\mathfrak{q}}
for large
t
, and
F(t,x,p)=\exp(-|a(t)^{2}p|)
a Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium.

Further, for any
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,1]
, there are other matter models for which the Einstein equations (
1.5
) admit as solutions the FLRW spacetime with spatial topology
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, and scale factor
a(t)=t^{\mathfrak{q}}
. These are the geometric backgrounds we consider in this article. We refer to the appendix of
[
84
]
for more information about how these spacetimes arise as solutions to the Einstein equations coupled to suitable matter models.

1.3.
The massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetimes

Let us now focus on the massless Boltzmann equation (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) on the FLRW spacetimes with
\mathbb{T}^{3}
spatial topology of the form

(1.6)

\mathcal{M}=(0,+\infty)_{t}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x},

with the Lorentzian metric

(1.7)

g_{\mathfrak{q}}=-dt\otimes dt+t^{2\mathfrak{q}}(dx^{1}\otimes dx^{1}+dx^{2}\otimes dx^{2}+dx^{3}\otimes dx^{3}),

where
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,1]
, and the speed of light is normalised to unity
c=1
. These geometric backgrounds for
\mathfrak{q}>0
model an expanding universe with a big bang singularity at
t=0
. For
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,1)
, these spacetimes are said to undergo decelerated expansion in the sense that the scale factor
a(t)=t^{\mathfrak{q}}
satisfies
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}a(t)<0
. The case
\mathfrak{q}=1
is linearly expanding in the sense that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}a(t)=0
. We refer to the spacetime (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) in the non-expanding case
\mathfrak{q}=0
, as the non-expanding FLRW spacetime. We will also consider this latter spacetime.

In this paper, we investigate the long-time dynamics of the
massless Boltzmann equation on the FLRW spacetime

(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
which takes the form

(1.8)

p^{0}\partial_{t}F+p^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}F-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}p^{0}\partial_{p^{i}}F=C(F,F),\qquad F(t=1,x,p)=F_{1}(x,p),

where
F_{1}(x,p)
is a regular initial datum, and
p^{0}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|
by the mass-shell relation with
|p|^{2}=(p^{1})^{2}+(p^{2})^{2}+(p^{3})^{2}
. Here, we have used the explicit form of the non-zero Christoffel symbols
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha}
of
g_{\mathfrak{q}}
given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{i}_{j0}=\Gamma^{i}_{0j}=\frac{\mathfrak{q}}{t}\delta_{ij},\quad\Gamma^{0}_{ij}=\frac{\mathfrak{q}}{t}t^{2\mathfrak{q}}\delta_{ij},

where
\delta_{ij}
is the standard Kronecker delta which is 1 when the indices are equal, and 0 otherwise. Note that for any smooth function
h(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)
, the linear operator in the left hand side of (
1.8
) satisfies

(1.9)

\displaystyle\Big(\partial_{t}+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}\Big)\big(h(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)\big)=0.

On the FLRW background (
1.6
)–(
1.7
), the collision operator
C(F,F)
is

(1.10)

C(F,G)=t^{9\mathfrak{q}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{dq}{q^{0}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{dq^{\prime}}{p^{\prime 0}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{dp^{\prime}}{p^{\prime 0}}W(p,q\,|p^{\prime},q^{\prime})[F(p^{\prime})G(q^{\prime})-F(p)G(q)],

in terms of the explicit volume form
d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}(p)=t^{3\mathfrak{q}}(p^{0})^{-1}dp^{1}dp^{2}dp^{3}
on the fibers of the mass-shell
\mathcal{P}
of FLRW.
Here, the transition rate
W(p,q|p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is given by (
1.3
).

1.3.1.
Scattering kernel of the collision operator

The scattering collision kernel
\sigma(\varrho,\theta)
we will consider in the transition rate
W(p,q|p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is

(1.11)

\sigma(\varrho,\theta)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\text{constant},

with value normalised to be 1. This kernel corresponds to the case of short range interactions
[
66
,
25
]
. We refer to the kernel (
1.11
) as the
hard-ball cross section
since it is the relativistic analogue of the hard-sphere kernel for the Newtonian Boltzmann equation.
1
1

1

The Newtonian limit of the Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
in the massive case with (
1.11
) is the usual hard-sphere Boltzmann equation as shown in
[
76
]
.

More general commonly studied examples of collision kernels include
\sigma(\varrho,\theta)
of the form

(1.12)

\sigma(\varrho,\theta)=\varrho^{a}\sigma_{0}(\theta),\quad\quad-4<a\leq 2,

for a suitable non-negative function
\sigma_{0}(\theta)
. We expect our techniques to generalize to these kernels with a suitable cutoff assumption on
\sigma_{0}(\theta)
. One example that appears frequently in the physics literature is the Israel kernel
[
44
]
:

(1.13)

\sigma(\varrho,\theta)=\frac{\sigma_{0}(\theta)}{\varrho(1+\varrho^{2})}.

We refer to
[
69
]
for more information. Other collision kernels are discussed for example in
[
76
, Appendix B]
, and
[
17
,
19
]
. See Section
1.9
for a further discussion about kernels of the form (
1.12
).

1.3.2.
Conservation laws and the H-theorem

Key quantities for the solutions of the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime (
1.8
) are their
mass
,
momentum
, and
energy
defined respectively as

(1.14)

t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}Fdpdx,\qquad t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p^{i}Fdpdx,\qquad t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|Fdpdx,

where
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Here the weight
t^{6\mathfrak{q}}
comes from the volume form of phase space. A basic property of the solutions to the massless Boltzmann equation (
1.8
) is the conservation in time of these quantities,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bigg(t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p^{i}\\
|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|\end{pmatrix}F(t,x,p)dpdx\bigg)

\displaystyle=0.

See Corollary
A.2
for the proof of these properties. These global conservation laws arise from the conservation of energy and momentum of collisions among particles.

We also observe that Boltzmann’s H-theorem holds in this setting. Let
H[F]
be the
entropy
of a distribution function
F(t,x,p)
at time
t
defined by

(1.15)

H[F]\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}-t^{6\mathfrak{q}}F\log F(t,x,p)dpdx.

where
-F\log F
is the standard entropy density. Similarly as in (
1.14
), the term
t^{6\mathfrak{q}}
comes from the volume form of phase space. In this context, the H-theorem states that the entropy of the system
H[F(t)]
is a non-decreasing function of time since

\frac{d}{dt}H[F(t)]\geq 0.
See Theorem
A.3
for its proof.

1.3.3.
Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria for massless Boltzmann on FLRW

In the massless case when
m=0
, the Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
has an explicit non-stationary
Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium

(1.16)

J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)=\frac{e^{-t^{\mathfrak{q}}p^{0}}}{8\pi}=\frac{e^{-|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|}}{8\pi},

where the normalising constant is chosen so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}J(p)dp=1
at
t=1
. For
\mathfrak{q}>0
the Maxwell–Jüttner solution (
1.16
) is decaying as
t\to+\infty
and thus referred to as a “non-stationary equilibrium”. In the massive case
m>0
, there are no non-trivial global Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria, see
[
12
, Section 12.5]
and
[
24
, Theorem 5.1]
.

More generally, the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime (
1.8
) admits an explicit
5
-dimensional family of non-stationary Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria,

(1.17)

J_{a,b,c}(t^{{2\mathfrak{q}}}p)=\frac{1}{8\pi}e^{a+b\cdot t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p-c|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|}

where
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
c>|b|
are five constant parameters. Note that
J_{0,0,1}=J
is the normalised Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium in (
1.16
).

The Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria
J_{a,b,c}
are characterised as
suitable maximisers of the entropy (
1.15
), subject to constant mass, momentum, and energy (
1.14
).

For
\mathfrak{q}=0
it is well known that the Boltzmann equation is dissipative around the stationary solution (
1.16
).
2
2

2

This fact is well-known in the case of massive particles. It is also true in the massless case as it is shown in Theorem
1.4
in the hard-ball case.
For
\mathfrak{q}>0
, since (
1.16
) is decaying in time, this dissipative effect weakens as
\mathfrak{q}
increases. This fact is captured in the weakening of the decay rates of the perturbations in Theorem
1.4
as
\mathfrak{q}
increases.

1.3.4.
The vacuum solution for massless Boltzmann on FLRW

For all
\mathfrak{q}\geq 0
, the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
admits the trivial stationary solution
F\equiv 0
, also known as the
vacuum solution
. This distribution function is referred as vacuum since it models a system with no particles. Theorem
1.9
below concerns the stability of the vacuum for the massless Boltzmann equation when
\mathfrak{q}>\frac{1}{3}
.

1.4.
The stability problem for the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium

In this work, we study the problem of convergence to equilibrium for the solutions of the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW (
1.8
). We will begin with a standard reformulation of the initial value problem around the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium (
1.16
). In what follows, we will use the notation

Q(F,G)=\frac{1}{p^{0}}C(F,G).

1.4.1.
Reformulation of the problem

Given a solution
F(t,x,p)
of (
1.8
), we define
f(t,x,p)
by the relation

(1.18)

\displaystyle F(t,x,p)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)+\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)}f(t,x,p).

The unknown
f(t,x,p)
satisfies the equation

(1.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}f-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}f+\mathcal{L}f=\Gamma(f,f),

with initial datum

(1.20)

f(t=1,x,p)=f_{1}(x,p)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}(F_{1}(x,p)-J(p))/\sqrt{J(p)}.

Here, the linearised operator
\mathcal{L}
and the nonlinear operator
\Gamma
are given by

(1.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}-J^{-1/2}Q(J,\sqrt{J}f)-J^{-1/2}Q(\sqrt{J}f,J),

and

(1.22)

\displaystyle\Gamma(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}J^{-1/2}Q(\sqrt{J}h_{1},\sqrt{J}h_{2}).

We will suppose in the following that the perturbation
f(t,x,p)
satisfies for all
t\geq 1
that

(1.23)

t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}J^{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)f(t,x,p)dpdx=t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p^{i}J^{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)f(t,x,p)dpdx\\
=t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|J^{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)f(t,x,p)dpdx=0,

where
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Due to the conservation of the mass, momentum, and energy, if these relations are satisfied initially then the equation (
1.19
) implies that they will continue to be satisfied for all
t\geq 1
.

For the stability problem of the more general Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria (
1.17
), one can reformulate the initial value problem similarly as in (
1.19
)–(
1.20
), and assume a condition on the perturbation
f(t,x,p)
similar to (
1.23
). The results we will obtain for the problem (
1.19
)–(
1.20
) can be extended to these other equilibria.

We could also relax the assumption (
1.23
) by identifying the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium
J_{a,b,c}
one would converge at large-times from the values of the conserved quantities (
1.14
) at
\{t=1\}
. See Appendix
B
. Then one can suitably modify the assumption (
1.23
) by the difference with the corresponding equilibrium
J_{a,b,c}
.

1.5.
The collision operator on FLRW

By the conservation of energy and momentum (
1.4
), we can parameterize
{p}^{\prime\mu}
and
{q}^{\prime\mu}
by
{p},{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and an angular variable
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Due to the diagonal structure of the FLRW metric, we can follow the arguments in the special relativistic case, for example in
[
77
]
, to reduce the collision integrals in (
1.10
). We present in this section the center of momentum and Glassey–Strauss reductions of the collision operator.

1.5.1.
Collisional invariants for massless particles on FLRW

Let us write the functions
s(p,q)
and
\varrho^{2}(p,q)
using the explicit form of the FLRW metric
g_{\mathfrak{q}}
in the case of massless particles
m=0
,

(1.24)

\displaystyle\varrho(p,q)=t^{\mathfrak{q}}\sqrt{2\big(|p||q|-p\cdot q\big)},\qquad s(p,q)=t^{2\mathfrak{q}}2\big(|p||q|-p\cdot q\big).

Note that
s=\varrho^{2}\geq 0.
We further show some elementary bounds for
s=\varrho^{2}
.

Lemma 1.1
.

For all
(t=x^{0},x)\in\mathcal{M}
and all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{P}_{x}
, there holds

(1.25)

t^{\mathfrak{q}}\frac{|p\times q|}{|p|^{\frac{1}{2}}|q|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\varrho(p,q)\leq t^{\mathfrak{q}}|p-q|,\qquad t^{2\mathfrak{q}}\frac{|p\times q|^{2}}{|p||q|}\leq s(p,q)\leq t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|p-q|^{2}.

Proof.

The estimates from above follow from

\displaystyle|p||q|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(|p|^{2}+|q|^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}|p-q|^{2}+p\cdot q.

On the other hand, the estimates from below follow from

(1.26)

s=2t^{2\mathfrak{q}}\frac{|p|^{2}|q|^{2}-(p\cdot q)^{2}}{|p||q|+p\cdot q}=2t^{2\mathfrak{q}}\frac{|p\times q|^{2}}{|p||q|+p\cdot q},

and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.
∎

The estimates in (
1.25
) are similar to those used for the massive case
m=1
in the non-expanding case
\mathfrak{q}=0
in
[
34
, Lemma 3.1]
.

1.5.2.
Center of momentum reduction of the collision operator

Let us reduce the collision operator
Q(F,G)
in the center-of-momentum representation. For this, we will use the parametrisation of the post-collisional momenta in the
center-of-momentum expression
written, for
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, as

(1.27)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{p+q}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{2(|p||q|-p\cdot q)}}{{2}}\bigg(\omega+(\gamma-1)(p+q)\frac{(p+q)\cdot\omega}{|p+q|^{2}}\bigg),

(1.28)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{p+q}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{2(|p||q|-p\cdot q)}}{{2}}\bigg(\omega+(\gamma-1)(p+q)\frac{(p+q)\cdot\omega}{|p+q|^{2}}\bigg),

where

\displaystyle\gamma=\gamma(p,q)=\frac{|p|+|q|}{\sqrt{2(|p||q|-p\cdot q)}}.

With this parametrisation of the post-collisional momenta

p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle|p^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\frac{|p|+|q|}{2}+\frac{(p+q)\cdot\omega}{2},\qquad|q^{\prime}|=\frac{|p|+|q|}{2}-\frac{(p+q)\cdot\omega}{2}.

Then, the conservation of energy and momentum (
1.4
) are satisfied in the form

(1.29)

|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|+|t^{\mathfrak{q}}q|=|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p^{\prime}|+|t^{\mathfrak{q}}q^{\prime}|,\qquad p+q=p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}.

Furthermore the scattering angle
\theta
satisfies

\displaystyle\cos\theta=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \omega\cdot k(p,q),

where

\displaystyle k^{i}(p,q)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{|p|+|q|+{\sqrt{2\left(|p||q|-p\cdot q\right)}}}\left(\frac{2\left(|q|p^{i}-|p|q^{i}\right)}{{\sqrt{2\left(|p||q|-p\cdot q\right)}}}+\left(p^{i}-q^{i}\right)\right).

It can be calculated that
|k|=1
.

Then, following the proof of
[
77
, Theorem 2 and Corollary 3]
, the collision operator
Q(F,G)
can be reduced in its
center-of-momentum representation
to

(1.30)

Q(F,G)=t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{dq}\penalty\ \int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}d\omega\penalty\ v_{\text{\o}}\sigma(\varrho,\theta)\penalty\ \big[F(p^{\prime})G(q^{\prime})-F(p)G(q)\big].

where
v_{\text{\o}}=v_{\text{\o}}(p,q)
is the Møller velocity given by

(1.31)

v_{\text{\o}}(p,q)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\frac{\varrho(p,q)s(p,q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4p^{0}q^{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|p||q|-p\cdot q}{|p||q|}=\frac{1}{4}\left|\frac{p}{|p|}-\frac{q}{|q|}\right|^{2}.

We used here that for massless particles in FLRW, we have
p^{0}=t^{\mathfrak{q}}|p|
,
q^{0}=t^{\mathfrak{q}}|q|
, and
\varrho^{2}=s=2t^{2\mathfrak{q}}\left(|p||q|-p\cdot q\right).

1.5.3.
Glassey–Strauss reduction of the collision operator

On the other hand, we can reduce the collision operator
Q(F,G)
in the Glassey–Strauss representation. For this, we will use the parametrisation of the post-collisional momenta in the Glassey–Strauss expression, for
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, as

(1.32)

\begin{split}p^{\prime}&=p+\frac{2\omega\cdot(|p|q-|q|p)(|p|+|q|)}{(|p|+|q|)^{2}-(\omega\cdot[p+q])^{2}}\omega,\\
q^{\prime}&=q-\frac{2\omega\cdot(|p|q-|q|p)(|p|+|q|)}{(|p|+|q|)^{2}-(\omega\cdot[p+q])^{2}}\omega.\end{split}

Then, following the proof of
[
77
, Theorem 4]
, the collision operator
Q(F,G)
can be reduced in the
Glassey–Strauss representation
as

(1.33)

Q(F,G)=t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{dq}\penalty\ \int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}d\omega\penalty\ v_{\text{\o}}\sigma(\varrho,\theta)\mathcal{B}({p},{q},\omega)\big[F(p^{\prime})G(q^{\prime})-F(p)G(q)\big],

where

\mathcal{B}({p},{q},{\omega})=\frac{4(|{p}|+|{q}|)^{2}\left|{\omega}\cdot(|{p}|{q}-|{q}|{p})\right|}{\left[(|{p}|+|{q}|)^{2}-({\omega}\cdot({p}+{q}))^{2}\right]^{2}}.

Recall the definition of the Møller velocity
v_{\text{\o}}
in (
1.31
).

By using the parametrisation (
1.32
) of the post-collisional momenta, following directly the argument in
[
33
]
, one can calculate the following Jacobian change of variables.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
be the post-collisional
momenta parametrised as in (
1.32
). For the collision map

(p,q)\mapsto(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
, the Jacobian change of variables is

(1.34)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}{\partial(p,q)}=-\frac{p^{0\prime}q^{0\prime}}{p^{0}q^{0}}=-\frac{|p^{\prime}||q^{\prime}|}{|p||q|}.

We use the parametrisation (
1.32
) to perform the standard pre-post collision change of variables
(p,q)\mapsto(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
using the Jacobian (
1.34
).

1.5.4.
Decomposition of the linearised operator

In the following, we will study the solutions
F(t,x,p)
of the massless Boltzmann equation on the FLRW spacetime

(1.35)

\partial_{t}F+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}F-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}F=Q(F,F),\qquad F(t=1,x,p)=F_{1}(x,p),

in the case of hard-ball cross section
\sigma=1
. Typically the center of momentum reduction of the collision operator will be used so that

(1.36)

Q(F,G)=\frac{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}dq\penalty\ \int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}d\omega\penalty\ \left(1-\frac{p\cdot q}{|p||q|}\right)\penalty\ \big(F(p^{\prime})G(q^{\prime})-F(p)G(q)\big),

where
p^{\prime}=p^{\prime}(p,q,\omega)
and
q^{\prime}=q^{\prime}(p,q,\omega)
are defined by (
1.27
)–(
1.28
).

We recall now the reformulation for the problem (
1.35
)–(
1.36
) around the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium as in (
1.19
). We now decompose the linearised operator
\mathcal{L}f
defined in (
1.21
) as

(1.37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}h

\displaystyle=\nu(t,p)h-\mathcal{K}(h),

in terms of the multiplication operator

(1.38)

\nu(t,p)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ v_{\text{\o}}\penalty\ \sigma(\varrho,\theta)\penalty\ J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q),

and the momentum integral operator
\mathcal{K}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}h

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ v_{\text{\o}}\penalty\ \sigma(\varrho,\theta)\penalty\ \sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q)}\left\{\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q^{\prime})}\penalty\ h(p^{\prime})+\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p^{\prime})}\penalty\ h(q^{\prime})\right\}

(1.39)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ v_{\text{\o}}\penalty\ \sigma(\varrho,\theta)\penalty\ \sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q)J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)}\penalty\ h(q)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{2}h-\mathcal{K}_{1}h,

where the last line defines the integral operators
\mathcal{K}_{2}
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
. We now compute the function
\nu(t,p)
, also known as collision frequency, for the hard-ball kernel.

Proposition 1.3
.

With the hard-ball cross section (
1.11
), the collision frequency is
\nu(t,p)=\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}
, where
\nu_{0}>0
is a fixed constant.

Proof.

By the explicit form (
1.31
) of the Møller velocity, we compute in the hard-ball cross-section case that

(1.40)

\displaystyle\nu(t,p)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}}{16\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ \left(1-\frac{p\cdot q}{|p||q|}\right)\penalty\ e^{-{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}}{16\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ \left(1-\frac{p\cdot q}{|p||q|}\right)\penalty\ e^{-{|q|}}=\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}},

where
\nu_{0}
is constant since the value of the integral does not depend upon
\frac{p}{|p|}
.
∎

1.6.
Notations and function spaces

Let us set the notations and function spaces we will use in the paper.

1.6.1.
Notations

Through the paper, we will write
A\lesssim B
if there is a uniform constant
C>0
such
that
A\leq CB
. If
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
, then we write
A\approx B
. We denote by
B_{r}=B(0,r)
the standard ball centered at zero and radius
r>0
. We let
\real{z}
and
\mathfrak{Im}\left(z\right)
denote the real and complex parts of a complex number
z
, respectively. In the Fourier variables
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, we also define the Japanese bracket

\displaystyle\langle k\rangle\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\left(1+|k|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The momentum of a particle is denoted by a 4-vector
p^{\mu}
. We raise and lower indices of the momenta with the FLRW metric, i.e.
p_{\mu}=g_{\mu\nu}p^{\nu}
. The components of the inverse of the metric
g
are denoted with indices upstairs as
g^{\mu\nu}
. Throughout the paper, Greek indices range over 0, 1, 2, 3, and lower case Latin indices range over 1, 2, 3.

We parameterize the momentum of a massless particle in FLRW spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
by
p=(p^{1},p^{2},p^{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with particle energy
p^{0}=t^{\mathfrak{q}}|p|
where
|p|^{2}=p\cdot p
. We write here and onwards the Euclidean dot product as
p\cdot q=\sum_{i=1}^{3}p^{i}q^{i}
. Abusively, we write
p^{\mu}
to denote both the 4-momentum
p^{\mu}=(p^{0},p^{i})
, and the component
p^{\mu}
.

1.6.2.
Weights and Lebesgue space norms

For
m\geq 0
, we define the weight function

(1.41)

\displaystyle w_{m}^{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}1+|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{m}.

For
r
,
s\geq 1
, we define the norms

\displaystyle\big\|h\big\|_{L^{r}_{p}}=\big\|h\big\|_{L^{r}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{p})}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}}\left|h(p)\right|^{r}dp\right)^{1/r},

\displaystyle\big\|h\big\|_{L^{s}_{T}}=\big\|h\big\|_{L^{s}([1,T])}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(\int_{1}^{T}\left|h(t)\right|^{s}dt\right)^{1/s},

\displaystyle\|h\|_{L^{s}_{k}}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{k}}\left|\hat{h}(k)\right|^{s}d\Sigma(k)\right)^{1/s},

where
\hat{h}(k)
is the Fourier transform of
h(x)
, that is,
\hat{h}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}e^{-ik\cdot x}h(x)dx
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. We used above the notation
d\Sigma(k)
for the discrete measure in
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}}h(k)\,d\Sigma(k)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}h(k).

In the following, we will use
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
to denote the standard
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p})
inner product.

1.6.3.
Weighted and mixed Lebesgue spaces

For
1\leq u
,
r<+\infty
, we consider the
L^{1}_{k}L^{u}_{T}L^{r}_{p}
mixed Lebesgue space norms

\|f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{u}_{T}L^{r}_{p}}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{k}}\bigg(\int_{1}^{T}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left|\hat{f}(t,k,p)\right|^{r}dp\bigg)^{\frac{u}{r}}dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{u}}d\Sigma(k),

the weighted mixed norms

\|w_{m}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{u}_{T}L^{r}_{p}}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{k}}\bigg(\int_{1}^{T}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}w_{m}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)^{r}\left|\hat{f}(t,k,p)\right|^{r}dp\bigg)^{\frac{u}{r}}dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{u}}d\Sigma(k),

and, for
l\geq 0
, the weighted norms

\|w_{m}f\|_{L^{1}_{k,l}L^{u}_{T}L^{r}_{p}}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{k}}\langle k\rangle^{l}\bigg(\int_{1}^{T}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}w_{m}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)^{r}\left|\hat{f}(t,k,p)\right|^{r}dp\bigg)^{\frac{u}{r}}dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{u}}d\Sigma(k).

We use the standard modification when
u
or
r
are equal to
+\infty
. Above,
\hat{f}(t,k,p)
denotes the Fourier transform of
f(t,x,p)
with respect to
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

1.6.4.
Chemin–Lerner function space

In the main results we consider the low regularity function space
L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}
. For
1\leq T<+\infty
, define the space of functions
L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}
with the norm

\|f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\esssup_{1\leq t\leq T}\|\hat{f}(t,k,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}\,d\Sigma(k).

For
\mathfrak{q}\in[0,1]
, our analysis will rely on estimates of the norm
\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}
for solutions of (
1.19
)–(
1.20
). The function space
L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}
was first considered for the study of collisional kinetic equations in
[
20
]
. In the rest of the paper when we write
\sup_{1\leq t\leq T}
we are referring to
\esssup_{1\leq t\leq T}
.

1.6.5.
Initial data norms

The initial data norms we will use are

\|f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{r}_{p}}(1)=\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{r}_{p}}\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{k}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left|\hat{f}_{1}(k,p)\right|^{r}dp\bigg)^{\frac{1}{r}}d\Sigma(k),

and

\|w_{m}f\|_{L^{1}_{k,l}L^{r}_{p}}(1)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{k}}\langle k\rangle^{l}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}w_{m}(p)^{r}\left|\hat{f}_{1}(k,p)\right|^{r}dp\bigg)^{\frac{1}{r}}d\Sigma(k).

When
l=0
the weighted initial data norm satisfies
\|w_{m}f\|_{L^{1}_{k,0}L^{r}_{p}}(1)=\|w_{m}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{r}_{p}}(1)
.

1.7.
The main results

We now present the main results of the article.

1.7.1.
Global stability of the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium

We now state our main result concerning the future global stability of the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium (
1.16
).

Theorem 1.4

(Future global stability of the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium)
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\in[0,1]
. The Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium is nonlinearly stable as a solution to the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime (
1.8
) in the case of hard-ball interaction (
1.11
).

More precisely, let
l\geq 0
and let
f_{1}(x,p)
be an initial datum satisfying (
1.23
). There is
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that if
F_{1}(x,p)=J(p)+J^{\frac{1}{2}}(p)f_{1}\geq 0
and
\|\langle k\rangle^{l}f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
, then there is a unique global mild solution
f(t,x,p)
to (
1.19
)–(
1.20
) for the massless Boltzmann equation with hard-ball interaction (
1.11
) on FLRW spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
such that
F(t,x,p)=J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)+J^{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)f(t,x,p)\geq 0
and

(1.42)

\displaystyle\forall\,T\geq 1,\qquad{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\langle k\rangle^{l}f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}\langle k\rangle^{l}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|\langle k\rangle^{l}{f}_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}.

Let also
m\geq 0
, and
0<\vartheta<1
. There is
\epsilon_{1}>0
such that if
\|w_{m}f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}\leq\epsilon_{1}
, then the unique global mild solution
f(t,x,p)
satisfies the uniform decay estimate

(1.43)

\displaystyle\forall\,t\geq 1,\qquad{\|{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{m/2}f(t)}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t))^{-1}\big\|w_{m}{f}_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}},

where

(1.44)

\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\begin{cases}\exp(\lambda t),\quad&\text{if}\,\,\mathfrak{q}=0,\\
\exp(\vartheta\frac{\nu_{0}}{1-3\mathfrak{q}}t^{1-3\mathfrak{q}}),\quad&\text{if}\,\,0<\mathfrak{q}<\frac{1}{3},\\
t^{\vartheta\nu_{0}},\qquad&\text{if}\,\,\mathfrak{q}=\frac{1}{3},\\
1,\qquad&\text{if}\,\,\frac{1}{3}<\mathfrak{q}\leq 1,\\
\end{cases}

with
\lambda>0
a small constant, and
\nu_{0}>0
the uniform constant in Proposition
1.3
.

Remark 1.5

(Functional space)
.

Alternatively, we could have obtained global stability in other function spaces such as

L^{\infty}_{T}H^{N}_{x}L^{2}_{p}\cap L^{2}_{T}H^{N}_{x}L^{2}_{p}
for
N>\frac{3}{2}
without using the Fourier transform, or other
L^{\infty}
based functional spaces. In the former case we expect the following uniform estimate

\displaystyle{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{N}_{x}L^{2}_{p}}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\big\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{N}_{x}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|{f}_{1}\big\|_{H^{N}_{x}L^{2}_{p}},\qquad\forall\,T\geq 1,

where the implicit constant is independent of
T\geq 1
.

Remark 1.6

(Asymptotic vs orbital stability)
.

By the explicit form of the Jüttner equilibrium, for any
m\geq 0
, one can show that
{\|{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{m}J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}}\sim t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}
for large times. In contrast, Theorem
1.4
establishes that for the norm of the perturbation
\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)}f(t,x,p)
we have
{\|{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{m}\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)}f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t))^{-1}
for large times. From (
1.44
),
t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t))^{-1}=t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}
for
1/3<\mathfrak{q}\leq 1
, and
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t))^{-1}(t)
decays faster in time for
0\leq\mathfrak{q}\leq 1/3
. In this sense, Theorem
1.4
can be viewed as orbital stability for
1/3<\mathfrak{q}\leq 1
, and asymptotic stability for
0\leq\mathfrak{q}\leq 1/3
.

Remark 1.7

(Dissipation threshold at
\mathfrak{q}=1/3
)
.

The
t^{3\mathfrak{q}}
decay rate in Theorem
1.4
should be viewed as a result of cosmological expansion. The additional decay factor in (
1.44
) for
\mathfrak{q}\leq 1/3
should be viewed as a result of dissipation due to particle collisions. Thus
\mathfrak{q}=1/3
is a threshold below which the Boltzmann equation is dissipative, and above which is not.

We expect different values of
\mathfrak{q}
arise as thresholds for different choices of collision kernels. See Section
1.9
below for more information.

Remark 1.8

(Cosmological expansion weakens familiar kinetic effects)
.

Note that the decay due to dissipation in Theorem
1.4
is stronger for small
\mathfrak{q}
, and thus cosmological expansion weakens this dissipative effect. A related weakening due to cosmological expansion of another familiar kinetic effect — namely phase mixing for the Vlasov equation — has also been shown to hold on FLRW spacetimes
[
84
]
.

1.7.2.
Global stability of the vacuum solution

We will now state our main result concerning the future global stability of the vacuum solution for the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW. Given a solution
F(t,x,p)
of the massless Boltzmann equation (
1.8
), we define
f(t,x,p)
by the relation

\displaystyle F(t,x,p)=J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)f(t,x,p),\qquad F(t=1,x,p)=F_{1}(x,p)=J(p)f_{1}(x,p).

The unknown
f
satisfies the equation

(1.45)

\partial_{t}f+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}f-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}f=\Gamma(f,f),

with initial datum

(1.46)

f(t=1,x,p)=f_{1}(x,p)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}F_{1}(x,p)/J(p).

Above in (
1.45
), the nonlinear term
\Gamma(f,f)
is defined as in (
1.22
). We now state our main result in the near vacuum case.

Theorem 1.9

(Future global stability of the vacuum solution)
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\in(\frac{1}{3},1]
. The vacuum is nonlinearly stable as a solution to the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime (
1.8
) in the case of hard-ball interaction (
1.11
).

More precisely, let
f_{1}(x,p)
be an initial datum. There is
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that if
F_{1}(x,p)=J^{\frac{1}{2}}(p)f_{1}\geq 0
and
\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
, then there is a unique global mild solution
f(t,x,p)
to (
1.45
)–(
1.46
) for the massless Boltzmann equation with hard-ball interaction (
1.11
) on FLRW spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
such that
F(t,x,p)=J^{\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)f(t,x,p)\geq 0
and

(1.47)

\forall T\geq 1,\qquad{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|f_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}.

Remark 1.10

(The restriction
\mathfrak{q}>\frac{1}{3}
)
.

The restriction
\mathfrak{q}>\frac{1}{3}
in Theorem
1.9
arises from the expansion rate used to estimate the nonlinear term in terms of the energy norm
\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}
, and is thus a priori unrelated to the appearance of
\mathfrak{q}=\frac{1}{3}
as a threshold in Theorem
1.4
.

Remark 1.11

(No previous near vacuum works on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
)
.

In the Newtonian Boltzmann theory, the standard approach near vacuum in the style of
[
43
]
uses the dispersion of the transport operator
\partial_{t}+p^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}
in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
to estimate the non-linear term. As far as we know, there are no global existence results near the vacuum solution, outside of homogeneity, on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}
for the Newtonian Boltzmann equation due to the lack of dispersion. (Note however recent developments on Landau damping for Vlasov–Poisson on
\mathbb{T}^{3}

[
62
,
7
,
36
]
.)

1.8.
Ideas and strategy of the proof

In this section, we overview the main steps of the proof of Theorem
1.4
. In Section
1.8.1
, we consider a linear model problem that motivates the time-decay rates we will show for the Boltzmann equation on FLRW depending on the value of
\mathfrak{q}
. Later, we will sketch the proof of our main results in Subsection
1.8.2
.

1.8.1.
A linear model problem

In this section, we will consider the model problem

(1.48)

\partial_{t}f+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}f-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}f+\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}f=0,\qquad f(t=1,x,p)=f_{1}(x,p).

This equation arises from neglecting nonlinear terms and the operator
\mathcal{K}
in the massless Boltzmann equation (recall Proposition
1.3
for the collision frequency in the hard ball case).

This is the simplest linear equation displaying the behaviour shown in Theorem
1.4
, and in particular does not admit the difficulties pertaining to the kernel of
\mathcal{L}
as in the full linearised problem. We will now study the decay properties of
\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}
for the solutions of (
1.48
).

Below it will be useful to note that for a function
h(t)
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

(1.49)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{dt}+\frac{\ell\mathfrak{q}}{t}\right)h(t)=t^{-\ell\mathfrak{q}}\frac{d}{dt}\left(t^{\ell\mathfrak{q}}h(t)\right).

Proposition 1.12

(Decay estimates for the model problem)
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\geq 0
and some
f_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Let
f
be the unique solution of the model problem (
1.48
). Then, the
L_{x,p}^{2}
-norm of
f
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall\,t\geq 1,\qquad{\|{f(t)}\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}}\leq t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\circ}(t))^{-1}\big\|{f}_{1}\big\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}},

where

\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\circ}(t)\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\begin{cases}\exp(t),\quad&\text{if}\,\,\mathfrak{q}=0,\\
\exp(\frac{\nu_{0}}{1-3\mathfrak{q}}t^{1-3\mathfrak{q}}),\quad&\text{if}\,\,0<\mathfrak{q}<\frac{1}{3},\\
t^{\nu_{0}},\qquad&\text{if}\,\,\mathfrak{q}=\frac{1}{3},\\
1,\qquad&\text{if}\,\,\frac{1}{3}<\mathfrak{q}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Multiplying (
1.48
) by
f
, and integrating over phase space, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}+\frac{3\mathfrak{q}}{t}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}+\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}=0.

We therefore obtain using (
1.49
) that

(1.50)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}t^{-6\mathfrak{q}}\frac{d}{dt}\left(t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}\right)+\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}=0.

The decay of the
L^{2}
-norm when
\mathfrak{q}=0
follows by integrating (
1.50
). Suppose now that
\mathfrak{q}\neq 0,

\frac{1}{3}
. We observe that
t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}
satisfies the ODE,

(1.51)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}\right)+\frac{2\nu_{0}}{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}}\left(t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}\right)=0,

which is explicitly solved by
\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}=t^{-6\mathfrak{q}}\exp(\frac{2\nu_{0}}{1-3\mathfrak{q}}t^{1-3\mathfrak{q}})\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}.
Finally, the decay estimate when
\mathfrak{q}=\frac{1}{3}
is obtained by integrating again (
1.51
) from which we conclude that
\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}=t^{-2-2\nu_{0}}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}^{2}.

∎

1.8.2.
Strategy of the proof

In this section we outline the proofs of Theorem
1.4
and Theorem
1.9
. The broad strategy of proof of Theorem
1.4
is familiar from previous works in the nonrelativistic and special relativistic settings
[
34
,
20
]
. However previous approaches would not be sufficient to close our estimates globally in time due to the
t^{2\mathfrak{q}}
rescaling from the cosmological expansion in the FLRW metric. More precisely in Section
2
we derive the macroscopic equations using a time-rescaled orthonormal momentum basis. This new approach is essential to obtaining the precise large time estimates for the solution with cosmological expansion. We also expect it to be useful in future developments. Furthermore we determine the precise effects of cosmological expansion on the large time behavior of solutions, and we develop new methodologies to overcome difficulties due to the masslessness of the particles. The proof of Theorem
1.9
relies solely on time decay that is caused by cosmological expansion.

The energy method
. The proof of Theorem
1.4
is based on Guo’s energy method
[
38
]
to control the perturbation
f
of the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium. We also use the low regularity Wiener algebra method using the Fourier transform from
[
20
]
. We will use the
energy norm

\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}
and the
dissipation norm

\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}
. The main energy estimate concerning these norms states that for all
T\geq 1
,

(1.52)

{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|{f}_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}},

which is established under the assumption that the initial perturbation is sufficiently small. From (
1.52
) global existence of the perturbation
f
can be shown by a standard continuity argument. We outline below the main arguments to derive (
1.52
).

The basic energy estimate.
Recall that the perturbation
f
satisfies equation (
1.19
) by the massless Boltzmann equation. Applying the Fourier transform in
x
to equation (
1.19
),

(1.53)

\left(\partial_{t}+i\frac{p}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\cdot k-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}\right)\hat{f}(t,k,p)+\mathcal{L}\hat{f}(t,k,p)=\widehat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{f})(t,k,p),

where
\widehat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{f})
corresponds to the nonlinearity of the system on the Fourier side (defined as in (
4.8
)). Then, an energy estimate can be obtained by taking the product of (
1.53
) with the complex conjugate
\bar{\hat{f}}
and taking the real part

(1.54)

\frac{1}{2}t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}(t,k)+\int^{t}_{1}\tau^{6\mathfrak{q}}\real{\left\langle{\mathcal{L}\hat{f}},{\hat{f}}\right\rangle}d\tau\\
=\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{f}_{1}(k,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{1}\tau^{6\mathfrak{q}}\real{\left\langle{\hat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{f})},{\hat{f}}\right\rangle}d\tau.

This energy estimate calls for a coercivity estimate for the linearised operator
\mathcal{L}
(defined by (
1.21
)).

Coercivity of the linearised operator
. Observe that the linearised operator
\mathcal{L}
has a 5-dimensional null space given by

{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})={\rm span}\left\{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)},\penalty\ \penalty\ t^{3\mathfrak{q}}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p^{i})\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)},\penalty\ \penalty\ t^{3\mathfrak{q}}|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)}\right\},

over smooth functions of
t
and
x
. The elements in
{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})
arise from the conservation laws of mass, energy, and momentum discussed in (
1.14
). For
\mathfrak{q}>0
, a new difficulty compared to previous works is that now the basis above is time-dependent.

Due to the non-trivial kernel, one can only hope to prove a coercivity estimate for
\mathcal{L}
modulo
{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})
. We will prove in Proposition
3.4
that there exists a uniform constant
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
h\in L^{2}_{p}
and all
t\geq 1
,

(1.55)

t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\langle\mathcal{L}h,h\rangle\geq\delta_{0}\|{\bf\{I-P\}}h\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}.

where
\mathbf{I}
is the identity operator on
L^{2}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{3}})
, and
{\bf P}
the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{3}})
onto
{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})
.

Recall the decomposition of the linearised operator

\mathcal{L}h=\nu h-\mathcal{K}h

in terms of the integral operator
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
1.39
), and the multiplication operator
\nu(t,p)=\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}
computed in Proposition
1.3
. The coercivity estimate (
1.55
) follows by using an explicit decomposition of
\mathcal{K}
into a small piece and a compact piece, using suitable cut-off functions in the integral form of the operator. Indeed, we show in Proposition
3.1
that for any small
\eta>0
we can explicitly decompose
\mathcal{K}
in terms of a compact operator
\mathcal{K}_{c}
by

(1.56)

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{c}+\mathcal{K}_{s},\qquad\text{where}\quad\|\mathcal{K}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{p}\to L^{2}_{p}}\leq\eta,

where
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{p}\to L^{2}_{p}}
is the operator norm from
L^{2}_{p}
to
L^{2}_{p}
.

Recall the decomposition
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{K}_{2}
in (
1.39
). Following the special relativistic calculation in
[
75
, Appendix]
or
[
17
]
, the operator
\mathcal{K}_{2}
can be written as

(1.57)

\mathcal{K}_{2}h=t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\penalty\ k_{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p,t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q)\penalty\ h(q),

in terms of the explicit kernel
k_{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p,t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)
given by

k_{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p,t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q)=\penalty\ 2c_{1}\bigg(1+\frac{|p|+|q|}{|p-q|}+2\frac{|p|+|q|}{t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|p-q|^{2}}\bigg)\left|\frac{p}{|p|}-\frac{q}{|q|}\right|^{2}\frac{e^{-t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|p-q|/2}}{t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|p-q|},

for some
c_{1}>0
. A general integral operator of the form (
1.57
) with kernel in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{q})
is compact, however
k_{2}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{q})
because of the decay at infinity and the singularity at
\{p=q\}
. With this in mind, we decompose
\mathcal{K}_{2}=\mathcal{K}_{2c}+\mathcal{K}_{2s}
in terms of operators defined by the following kernels,

k_{2c}(p,q)=\phi_{R,\epsilon}k_{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p,t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q),\qquad k_{2s}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p,t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q)=(1-\phi_{R,\epsilon})k_{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p,t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q),

where
\phi_{R,\epsilon}
is a cut-off supported in the domain
\{t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|p|+t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|q|\leq R\}\cap\{t^{2\mathfrak{q}}|p-q|\geq 2\epsilon\}
for constants
R>\epsilon>0
. The operator
\mathcal{K}_{2c}
is then compact since
k_{2c}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{q})
, and the operator norm of
\mathcal{K}_{2s}
is small for
R
sufficiently large and
\epsilon
sufficiently small. On the other hand, in this case of hard-ball interactions, the operator
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is compact since its kernel belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{q})
(however
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is also decomposed as (
3.6
) so that
\mathcal{K}_{c}
additionally satisfies the estimate (
3.1
)).

The coercivity estimate (
1.55
) can then be proved by contradiction using the decomposition (
1.56
) (see Proposition
3.4
). However, the coercivity estimate (
1.55
) only controls the perturbation
f
projected out of
{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})
. To close the energy estimate for the perturbation
f
, we will need further estimates for the projection
{\bf P}f
into
{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})
.

The macroscopic estimates
. In the following, we will decompose the perturbation as
f=\mathbf{P}f+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}f,
into its
macroscopic part

\mathbf{P}f
and
microscopic part

\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}f
. We write
\mathbf{P}f
as a linear combination of basis vectors

{\bf P}f=\left(\mathcal{A}^{f}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{B}^{f}_{i}t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p^{i}/2+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\mathcal{C}^{f}(\left|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p\right|-3)\right)t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\sqrt{J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)},

in terms of the coefficients
\mathcal{A}^{f}(t,x)
,
\mathcal{B}^{f}(t,x)
, and
\mathcal{C}^{f}(t,x)
, given by the explicit momentum averages of
f
stated in (
2.4
). Plugging the decomposition
f=\mathbf{P}f+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}f
into (
1.19
), we obtain

(1.58)

\left\{\partial_{t}+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}\right\}{\bf P}f=-\left\{\partial_{t}-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}\right\}{\bf\{I-P\}}f\\
-\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}({\bf\{I-P\}}f)-\mathcal{L}({\bf\{I-P\}}f)+\Gamma(f,f).

When the operator
\partial_{t}+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}
on the right hand side acts on
{\mathrm{nul}}(\mathcal{L})
, the result lives in a 13-dimensional space. Then, by considering a basis
\{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{e}_{j}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)\}_{j=1}^{13}
of this space, we will obtain a system of local conservation laws (
2.10
) and macroscopic equations (
2.11
) for the coefficients
\mathcal{A}^{f}
,
\mathcal{B}^{f}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{f}
. In particular, by the local conservation laws and (
1.23
), the coefficients have zero spatial average for all
t\geq 1
.

The system of macroscopic equations for
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(t,k)
,
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(t,k)
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}(t,k)
, is estimated on the Fourier side. Recall that the spatial average of the coefficients vanish,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(t,0)=\widehat{\mathcal{B}}(t,0)=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}(t,0)
for all
t\geq 1
. We first estimate
\widehat{C}
which satisfies the following schematic equations

(1.59)

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}t^{-\mathfrak{q}}ik_{j}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}+t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}\partial_{t}\left(t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{RHS}_{1},\qquad 1\leq j\leq 3,

(1.60)

\displaystyle t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}\partial_{t}\left(t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}\right)+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3}}t^{-\mathfrak{q}}ik\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{RHS}_{2},

where the right hand sides
\mathrm{RHS}_{1}
, and
\mathrm{RHS}_{2}
, are defined in terms of
L^{2}_{p}
-projections using the orthonormal functions
\{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{e}_{j}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)\}_{j=1}^{13}
considered earlier, as for example
\langle{\bf\{I-P\}}\hat{f},\,t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{e}_{\ell}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)\rangle
, and also nonlinear terms. Multiplying (
1.59
) by the term
-t^{4\mathfrak{q}}ik_{j}\overline{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(t,k)
, summing on
j
, and integrating in time, one obtains

(1.61)

\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\int_{1}^{T}t^{3\mathfrak{q}}|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}(t,k)|^{2}dt=\left.\frac{i}{|k|^{2}}\left(\left(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\cdot k\right)t^{4\mathfrak{q}}\overline{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(t,k)\right)\right|_{t=1}^{T}\\
-\frac{i}{|k|^{2}}\int_{1}^{T}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\cdot k\right)t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\partial_{t}\left(t^{\mathfrak{q}}\overline{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(t,k)\right)dt+\mathrm{RHS_{3}},

for some right hand side
\mathrm{RHS_{3}}
.
The first term on the right hand side can be bounded by
T^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|\hat{f}(T,k)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}
and
\|\hat{f}_{1}(k)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}
, whereas the second term can be bounded using (
1.60
) to estimate the term involving
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
. As a result, one can show

(1.62)

\int_{1}^{T}t^{3\mathfrak{q}}|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}(t,k)|^{2}dt\lesssim T^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|\hat{f}(T,k)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}+\big\|\hat{f}_{1}(k)\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{1}t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\left|\widehat{{\mathcal{B}}}(t,k)\right|^{2}dt\\
+\int_{1}^{T}t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\big\|{\bf\{I-P\}}\hat{f}(t,k)\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}dt+\mathrm{NLE},

where
\mathrm{NLE}
denotes non-linear errors. The second integral on the right hand side of (
1.62
) comes from estimating
\mathrm{RHS}_{3}
.
In a similar fashion, one can later estimate
\widehat{A}-\sqrt{3}\widehat{C}
, and finally
\widehat{B}
, exploiting an upper triangular structure in the system. The main estimate for the macroscopic coefficients is then

(1.63)

\begin{split}\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}]\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}}&\lesssim\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}{\bf\{I-P\}}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}+\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}\\
&\qquad+{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}},\end{split}

where
\|[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}]\|\overset{\mbox{\tiny{def}}}{=}\|\mathcal{A}\|+\|\mathcal{B}\|+\|\mathcal{C}\|
. The estimate (
1.63
) controls the dissipation norm of the macroscopic part since

(1.64)

\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}{\bf P}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}\lesssim\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}]\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}}.

Estimate of the nonlinear term
. To close the energy estimate, one needs to estimate the cubic nonlinear term on the right hand side of (
1.54
). We will prove an
L^{2}_{p}
-trilinear estimate in Lemma
3.6
according to which

|\langle\Gamma(f,h),\eta\rangle|\lesssim\big\|f\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\big\|h\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\big\|\eta\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})},

for all
f
,
h
,
\eta\in L^{2}_{p}
. By using this trilinear estimate on the Fourier side, one can conclude for all
f
,
h
, and
\eta\in L^{2}_{p}
that the following uniform estimate holds

(1.65)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle{\widehat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{h})(k)},{\hat{\eta}(k)}\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\big\|\hat{\eta}(k)\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\|\hat{f}(k-l)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}\big\|\hat{h}(l)\big\|_{L^{2}_{p}}\,d\Sigma(l).

This estimate can be used to estimate the nonlinear term in (
1.54
).

Global existence and uniform energy estimates
. We can then perform the main energy estimate for the perturbation
f
. By using the coercivity estimate (
1.55
) on top of the basic energy estimate (
1.54
),

\displaystyle t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}_{p}}(t,k)

\displaystyle+\left(\int^{t}_{1}\tau^{3\mathfrak{q}}\|{\bf\{I-P\}}\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}(\tau,k)d\tau\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\hat{f}_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{p}}(k)+\left(\int^{t}_{1}\tau^{6\mathfrak{q}}\left|{\left\langle{\hat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{f})},{\hat{f}}\right\rangle}\right|d\tau\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Then, by using the identity
\langle\Gamma(f,f),h\rangle=\langle\Gamma(f,f),{\bf\{I-P\}}h\rangle
on the Fourier side, together with the estimate (
1.65
) for the nonlinear term, one obtains

(1.66)

\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}{\bf\{I-P\}}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\\
\lesssim\|{f}_{1}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}+{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}},

for all
T\geq 1
. See Proposition
5.1
. Finally, applying the estimate (
1.63
)–(
1.64
) for the macroscopic part
{\bf P}f
, one can show that for all
T\geq 1
,

(1.67)

{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|{f}_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}.

The last term on the right hand side can be absorbed for a small initial perturbation, and (
1.52
) concluded. Global existence for the massless Boltzmann equation follows from this estimate and a standard continuity argument.

Improved energy estimates
. A posteriori we show improved time-decay rates for the perturbation
f
when
0\leq\mathfrak{q}\leq\frac{1}{3}
. These estimates rely on the computation of the collision frequency
\nu(t,p)=\nu_{0}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}
in Proposition
1.3
, and a perturbative treatment of the integral operator
\mathcal{K}
based on the compact-small decomposition (
1.56
). Indeed, we consider the equation satisfied by
t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t)\hat{f}
(with
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t)
defined as in (
1.44
)) and then perform a similar energy estimate to (
1.67
). We will show in Proposition
6.20
that

\displaystyle\forall t\geq 1,\qquad{\|{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t)f}\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}/2}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}(t)f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|{f}_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}.

A posteriori we show weighted energy estimates for
w_{m}\hat{f}
in terms of the weights
w_{m}^{2}(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)=1+|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{m}
, and propagate spatial regularity by performing energy estimates for
\langle k\rangle^{l}\hat{f}(t,k,p)
on the Fourier side. See Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.8
, respectively.

The proof of Theorem
1.9
. The near vacuum result is also based on the energy method. For this problem, the main energy estimate states that for all
T\geq 1
,

(1.68)

{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|f_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}.

The linearised operator around vacuum is trivial, and the estimate (
1.68
) is therefore established using the time decay arising from expansion only. In particular, previous difficulties regarding the kernel and coercivity of the linearised operator are not present anymore.

Recall that the perturbation
f
satisfies (
1.45
). Performing the basic energy estimate, for all
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}t^{6\mathfrak{q}}\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}(t,k)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}\|\hat{f}_{1}(k,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{p}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{1}\tau^{6\mathfrak{q}}\real{\left\langle{\hat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{f})},{\hat{f}}\right\rangle}d\tau.

The estimate (
1.68
) is then shown by estimating the time integral of the nonlinear term on the right hand side. Using the trilinear estimate (
1.65
) on the Fourier side, it can be shown that for all
\tau\geq 1
,

\tau^{6\mathfrak{q}}\left|\left\langle{\hat{\Gamma}(\hat{f},\hat{f})(\tau,k)},{\hat{f}(\tau,k)}\right\rangle\right|\\
\lesssim\tau^{-3\mathfrak{q}}{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\hat{f}(k)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\int_{\mathbb{Z}^{3}}{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\hat{f}(k-l)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}\hat{f}(l)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\,d\Sigma(l).

Assuming
\mathfrak{q}>\frac{1}{3}
, so that
\tau^{-3\mathfrak{q}}
is integrable, one can show that,

\displaystyle\forall T\geq 1,\qquad{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}\lesssim\big\|f_{1}\big\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}+{\big\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}{f}\big\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{k}L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{p}}}.

The last term on the right hand side can be absorbed for a small initial perturbation, and (
1.68
) concluded. Global existence follows from this estimate and a standard continuity argument.

1.9.
Discussion of more general collision kernels

In this section, we discuss scattering kernels of the form

(1.69)

\sigma(\varrho,\theta)=\varrho^{a}\sigma_{0}(\theta),\quad\quad-4<a\leq 2,

for an integrable non-negative function
\sigma_{0}(\theta)
. We first formulate a conjecture on the dissipation threshold for these more general kernels. We then discuss the behaviour of the associated model problem.

1.9.1.
Dissipation threshold for more general kernels

We expect the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium to be future nonlinearly stable for all kernels of the form (
1.69
). Recall (see Remark
1.7
) that, for
a=0
, expansion rate
\mathfrak{q}=1/3
is a threshold below which there is dissipation, and above which there is not. We expect a different threshold value of
\mathfrak{q}
to arise for the more general kernels in (
1.69
).

Conjecture 1.13

(Dissipation threshold at
(3+a)\mathfrak{q}=1
for
-3<a\leq 2
.)
.

Consider
\mathfrak{q}\in[0,1]
, and the massless Boltzmann equation with scattering kernel (
1.69
) on FLRW (
1.8
), (
1.10
). The Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium
J(t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p)
is future nonlinearly stable. Moreover, for any
\max\{3+a,0\}\mathfrak{q}\leq 1
the norm
\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}
decays uniformly as
t\to+\infty
— with quantitative rates, depending on
\mathfrak{q}
in (
1.7
) and on
a
in (
1.69
) — and in general the norm
\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}
does not decay for
\max\{3+a,0\}\mathfrak{q}>1
, where the perturbation
f
is defined by (
1.18
). Notably for
-4<a\leq-3
the norm
\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}
decays uniformly as
t\to+\infty
with quantitative rates for any
\mathfrak{q}\in[0,1].

In particular, for
a\in(-4,-1]
we expect that
t^{3/2}\|f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{k}L^{2}_{p}}
decays for solutions of the massless Boltzmann equation with scattering kernel (
1.69
) on FLRW with scale factor
\mathfrak{q}=\frac{1}{2}
(which satisfies the Einstein–massless Boltzmann system, as discussed in Section
1.2
). Moreover, in the case of Israel particles, in which
\sigma(\varrho,\theta)
is defined by (
1.13
), we expect
\mathfrak{q}=\frac{1}{2}
to be the dissipation threshold.

In the next section, we will consider a linear model problem that motivates the threshold scale factor
\max\{3+a,0\}\mathfrak{q}=1
considered earlier. From this model problem one can also extract quantitative decay rates, which are informative regarding the decay rates one may expect for the massless Boltzmann equation with these scattering kernels.

One may formally compare the above threshold behaviour with the Euler equations on FLRW where, for each linear equation of state, there is an expected threshold value of expansion rate
\mathfrak{q}
, below which shock waves form in perturbations of constant density fluids at rest, and above which such shock waves do not form
[
28
]
.

1.9.2.
Model problem for more general kernels

Motivated by the stability of the Maxwell–Jüttner equilibrium for the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{\mathfrak{q}})
in the case of scattering kernels of the form (
1.69
), we consider the following model problem for
a>-4

(1.70)

\partial_{t}f+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}f-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}f+\nu_{0}\frac{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{\frac{a}{2}}}{t^{(3+a)\mathfrak{q}}}f=0,\quad f(t=1,x,p)=f_{1}(x,p),

with
\nu_{0}>0
a fixed constant. We have written the last term using
t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p
since this weight belongs to the kernel of the transport operator
\partial_{t}+\frac{p^{i}}{|t^{\mathfrak{q}}p|}\partial_{x^{i}}-\frac{2\mathfrak{q}}{t}p^{i}\partial_{p^{i}}
in (
1.70
). This model problem should be compared with the problem (
1.48
) previously considered in the hard-ball case.

When considering more general scattering kernels, the linearised operator
\mathcal{L}
in the Boltzmann equation can also be decomposed as in (
1.37
) in terms of the multiplication operator
\nu(t,p)
in (
1.38
). We now compute
\nu(t,p)
for the kernel (
1.69
).

Proposition 1.14
.

For scattering kernels of the form (
1.69
) with an integrable function
\sigma_{0}(\theta)
, the collision frequency (
1.38
) is

\nu(t,p)=\nu_{0}\frac{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{\frac{a}{2}}}{t^{(3+a)\mathfrak{q}}},

where
\nu_{0}
is a fixed constant depending on
a
.

Proof.

By the explicit form (
1.31
) of the Møller velocity in FLRW,

\displaystyle\nu(t,p)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{t^{3\mathfrak{q}}}{16\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ \left(1-\frac{p\cdot q}{|p||q|}\right)\penalty\ \varrho^{a}\sigma_{0}(\theta)e^{-{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}q|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{t^{-3\mathfrak{q}}}{16\pi}|p|^{\frac{a}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\penalty\ dq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\penalty\ d\omega\penalty\ \sigma_{0}(\theta)\penalty\ \left(1-\frac{p\cdot q}{|p||q|}\right)\penalty\ \left(|q|-\frac{p}{|p|}\cdot q\right)^{\frac{a}{2}}e^{-{|q|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \nu_{0}|p|^{\frac{a}{2}}t^{-3\mathfrak{q}},

in terms of a uniform positive constant
\nu_{0}
depending on
a
.
∎

The model problem (
1.70
) then arises from neglecting nonlinear terms and the operator
\mathcal{K}
in the massless Boltzmann equation (
1.19
). This problem is distinguished from the more well known cases of
(I)
the massive special relativistic Boltzmann equation and
(II)
the Newtonian Boltzmann equation by at least two complicating factors. First, the massless case presents a degeneracy when
a>0
for small values of
|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|
in the damping factor
{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{\frac{a}{2}}}t^{-(3+a)\mathfrak{q}}
. Second, the damping is degenerating for large
t
with
t^{-(3+a)\mathfrak{q}}
. For the soft potentials
a<0
, this degeneration is diluted by small values of
|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|
in an energy norm, which makes
{|t^{2\mathfrak{q}}p|^{\frac{a}{2}}}
large, and thereby increases the damping rate. Note one can show using techniques from
[
79
]
the following decay rates for the
L^{2}
norm of solutions
f
to (
1.70
).

Proposition 1.15

(Decay estimates for general model problem)
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\in[0,1]
,
-4<a\leq 2
,
s>0
, and
f_{1}
a sufficiently regular initial data. If either
-3<a\leq 2
and
(3+a)\mathfrak{q}<1
or
-4<a\leq-3
and
\mathfrak{q}\in[0,1]
, then the unique solution
f
of the model problem (
1.70
) satisfies

\displaystyle\forall\,t\geq 1,\qquad\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}\lesssim e^{-Ct^{d}}\|e^{\frac{{\nu_{0}}}{s}(2s+|a|)|p|^{s}}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}},

where
d=\frac{2s}{2s+|a|}\left(1-(3+a)\mathfrak{q}\right)\in[0,1)
, and
C>0
is a uniform constant. Otherwise, for
-3<a\leq 2
and
(3+a)\mathfrak{q}=1
the unique solution
f
of (
1.70
) satisfies

\displaystyle\forall\,t\geq 1,\qquad\|t^{3\mathfrak{q}}f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-C\left(\log(t)\right)^{\frac{2s}{2s+|a|}}}\|e^{\frac{{\nu_{0}}}{s}(2s+|a|)|p|^{s}}f_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x,p}},

where
C>0
is a possibly different uniform constant.

This behavior is very different from the massive cases
(I)
and
(II)
when
\mathfrak{q}=0
. In those cases the soft potentials
a<0
are well known to substantially reduce the damping rate from exponential to sub-exponential. Specifically in those other cases one expects exponential time decay for
a\geq 0
as in
[
85
,
34
]
, and one only expects rapid polynomial or super-polynomial decay for
a<0
as in
[
75
,
78
,
79
,
10
,
11
]
. Thus we see a
reduction
in the time decay rates when we
reduce
the value of
a
in
(I)
and
(II)
. In contrast in Proposition
1.15
above we have an
improvement
in the range of
\mathfrak{q}>0
for which the fastest superpolynomial decay rates hold when
reducing
the value of
a
. Specifically, for
-4<a\leq-3
, in Proposition
1.15
we obtain the superpolynomial decay rates for any value of
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,1]
.

1.10.
Related works

Let us present here a nonexhaustive overview of related previous works.

1.10.1.
Collisional kinetic theory in general relativity

The formulation of the kinetic theory of gases in general relativity began with the early contributions of Synge
[
81
]
in 1934, and Walker
[
87
]
in 1936. See also the work by Einstein
[
23
]
in 1939. Concerning the collisional theory, the Boltzmann equation was derived in special relativity by Lichnerowicz–Marrot
[
57
]
in 1940, and in general relativity by Chernikov
[
13
,
14
]
in 1961. Around the same time Israel derived his well-known collision kernel for the relativistic Boltzmann equation
[
44
]
in 1963. The collisional theory was further developed in works by Lindquist
[
58
]
in 1966, Bel
[
8
]
in 1969, Ehlers
[
22
]
in 1969, Ehlers–Sachs
[
70
]
in 1971, among other. Later physics references include the works of Dijkstra–Van Leeuwen
[
18
,
19
]
in 1978, Naumov
[
64
]
in 1982, Ray
[
67
]
in 1982, Hiscock–Salmonson
[
42
]
in 1991, and Kremer
[
46
,
47
]
around 2012. See also the introductions to relativistic kinetic theory by Sarbach–Zannias
[
71
]
from 2013, and Acuña-Cárdenas–Gabarrete–Sarbach
[
1
]
from 2022. For textbook references, we refer to van Weert–De Groot–van Leeuwen
[
17
]
from 1980, to Bernstein
[
9
]
from 1988, and to Cercignani–Kremer
[
12
]
from 2002.

1.10.2.
Local well-posedness for the Einstein–Boltzmann system

Regarding rigorous mathematical treatments of the full Einstein–Boltzmann system, note the work of Bancel–Choquet-Bruhat
[
5
]
on local well-posedness from 1973; this result builds upon
[
15
,
4
]
. Later, Rendall–Lee
[
55
]
revisited the problem of local well-posedness for Einstein–Boltzmann in 2013. For textbook references about the Einstein–Boltzmann system see the books by Rendall
[
68
]
from 2008, Choquet-Bruhat
[
16
]
from 2009, and Ringström
[
69
]
from 2013.

1.10.3.
Global existence of equilibria for the Boltzmann equation in special relativity

For the mathematical work on the asymptotic stability of Maxwell–Jüttner equilibria for the Boltzmann equation without symmetry assumptions, there have been several works on fixed backgrounds. Glassey–Strauss
[
34
]
proved the stability of equilibria for the relativistic Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}
with hard interactions in 1993. Glassey–Strauss
[
31
]
extended that result to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
in 1995. Later, the relativistic Boltzmann equation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
with soft interactions was addressed by Strain–Zhu
[
80
]
in 2012. The problem of deriving energy estimates with momentum regularity in these settings was resolved by Guo–Strain
[
40
]
in 2012.

More recently, a definitive stability of the Maxwellian equilibrium for the relativistic Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}
without angular cut-off was obtained by Jang–Strain
[
45
]
in 2022. In the non-relativistic case, nonlinear stability for the Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{x}
without angular cut-off was shown earlier in
[
37
]
by Gressman–Strain in 2011. This stability problem in the non-relativistic case was later revisited by Duan–Liu–Sakamoto–Strain
[
19
]
who obtained global existence in a low regularity Chemin–Lerner function space. In the present article, the function space we use is motivated by that function space. Global existence of Maxwellians for the non-relativistic Boltzmann equation has been studied in many other works. See, for example,
[
2
,
3
,
72
,
59
,
21
,
86
,
39
]
and the references therein.

We also comment the result on the Hilbert expansion for the relativistic Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
by Speck–Strain
[
74
]
in 2011.

1.10.4.
Global existence results for the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation in cosmology

The Boltzmann equation and the Einstein–Boltzmann system have both been studied previously in cosmology under symmetry assumptions. For certain kernels, for large data, global existence for spatially homogeneous FLRW solutions of the Einstein–Boltzmann system with a positive cosmological constant was obtained by Noutchegueme–Takou
[
65
]
in 2006. Later, the asymptotic behaviour of these solutions was studied by Takou
[
82
]
in 2009. On fixed Minkowski and FLRW backgrounds, large data global existence for spatially homogeneous solutions of the Boltzmann equation with certain hard interactions was studied by Lee–Rendall
[
56
]
in 2013.

Also, small data global existence for the Boltzmann equation and the Einstein–Boltzmann system have been considered. Global existence near vacuum for the Einstein–Boltzmann system with Bianchi I symmetry was shown by Lee–Nungesser
[
51
]
in 2017. Later, small data global existence for the Einstein–Boltzmann–scalar field system with Bianchi I-VII symmetry for Israel particles was shown by Lee–Lee–Nungesser
[
50
]
in 2023. There is additionally the work of Lee which considers a spatially-homogeneous Newtonian cosmological setting
[
48
]
from 2016. We also refer to the global existence of homogeneous solutions for the Einstein–Boltzmann system with a positive cosmological constant by Lee–Nungesser
[
52
]
in the case of certain soft interactions in 2024.

1.10.5.
Works on the spatially homogeneous massless Boltzmann equation in cosmology

We discuss related works for massless Boltzmann in cosmology. Small data global existence for the spatially homogeneous massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW spacetimes was shown by Lee
[
49
]
for some collision kernels in a range of soft and hard potentials in 2021. Note also the work
[
83
]
on spatially homogeneous FLRW solutions with
\mathfrak{q}=1/2
for the Einstein–massless Vlasov system with spatial topology
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}
in spherical symmetry. For data posed at the initial singularity, Lee–Nungesser–Tod
[
54
]
proved finite-time existence for the massless Boltzmann equation on FLRW backgrounds for some soft scattering kernels in 2020. Together also with Stalker
[
53
]
, this result was extended to Bianchi I spacetimes in 2024.

1.10.6.
Works on other equations on FLRW spacetimes

There have been several works on other related equations on FLRW. It was shown recently by Taylor–Velozo Ruiz
[
84
]
that a phase mixing effect occurs for the Vlasov equation on FLRW. See Remark
1.8
. See also
[
30
]
for the study of a related nonlinear, non-relativistic model.

There have been several works
[
29
,
28
,
73
]
concerned with the future stability of homogeneous solutions for the Euler equations on FLRW spacetimes. In the recent work
[
28
]
by Fajman–Maliborski–Ofner–Oliynyk–Wyatt it is suggested that shock formation occurs for slowly expanding spacetimes. See also
[
27
]
.

There have also been many works on the linear wave equation on FLRW. In the decelerated regime, for the case where the spatial slices are flat copies of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the combined effects of dispersion and expansion are relevant. See, for example, the recent articles of Natário–Rossetti
[
63
]
and Haghshenas
[
41
]
and references within.

1.11.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, the distribution function is decomposed in its microscopic and macroscopic parts. Moreover, the system of macroscopic equations is derived. In Section
3
, the main estimates for the collision operator are performed. In particular, the coercivity estimate for the linearised operator is shown. In Section
4
, the system of equations derived in Section
2
is used to estimate the macroscopic part of the distribution. In Section
5
, the energy estimates for the full solution
f
are shown. The proof of Theorem
1.4
is completed in Section
6
, and the proof of Theorem
1.9
is given in Section
7
. Appendix
A
contains the proof of the H-theorem and the conservation of mass, energy, and momentum, discussed in Section
1.3.2
. In Appendix
B
, the Maxwell–Jüttner parameters are identified for fixed values of the initial mass, energy, and momentum.

1.12.
Acknowledgements
